Introduction
An attempt to produce an English translation of the entire Cologne Mani-Codex by Judith and Sam Lieu was begun in the early 1980s. The intention then was to produce a translation suitable for university teaching based on the editio major of Albert Henrichs and Ludwig Koenen.1 Judith Lieu was responsible for translating pp. 1-99 of the codex and Samuel Lieu, the rest. Their joint translation which was checked against editio minor of Albert Henrichs, Ludwig Koenen and Cornelia Römer2, was excerpted in Iain Gardener and Samuel N.C. Lieu (ed.), Manichaean Texts from the Roman Empire (Cambridge, 2004). The generous gift by the late Ludwig Koenen of an electronic version of the editio minor to the Database of Manichaean Texts Project meant that a version of the Cologne Mani-Codex with a standard Greek text accompanied by the translation of Lieu and Lieu for the Series Graeca of the Corpus Fontium Manichaeorum was on the horizon but sadly there it remained for the next few decades. The Greek font used for the electronic version of the editio minor was obsoleted by the advent of Unicode and it took a Herculean effort to reproduce in Unicode the exact same text as presented in the editio minor.
The present work is an on-going effort to produce a standard Greek text with English translation of this unique parchment codex and one of the most important primary sources of Manichaeism. It contains an English translation by Mark Vermes of the 1988 Foreword of the editio minor together with the Greek text as presented in that same volume. The English translation, based on that of Lieu and Lieu, has been thoroughly revised by Mark Vermes. The translation is not accompanied by a historical commentary as in the editio minor of 1988 - this will come as a pars secunda - but by textual notes showcasing improvements made to the text especially by Dr (now Prof.) Cornelia Römer. A Greek lexical index has been added for the convenience of researchers.
The editors and translators of this volume are grateful to Associate Professor Paul McKechnie of Macquarie University for much helpful advice on the English translation of the Greek text of the Codex.
Samuel N.C. Lieu FBA 25.9.2024

Foreword (1988)
1.
The Cologne Mani Codex is a parchment-book in an unusually small format (3.8 x 4.5 cm) and tiny but very careful and legible writing. It can be dated to the end of the fourth or the fifth century AD. The site where it was found is unknown, but is probably in Upper Egypt. The text was probably translated in Egypt from East Aramaic into Greek around the middle of the fourth century.1 Under the title περί της γέννης του σώματος αυτού ("On the development of his body")? it contains a biography of Mani. This was probably assembled by a compiler in the first third of the fourth century from larger and smaller parts, each of which is quoted under the name of one or more sources.3 These excerpts mostly quote autobiographical statements of Mani. The informants [p. XVI] had collected and reported in their own works what they had learned about the life of the founder of their religion and about his pronouncements. The compiler carefully arranged all of this into a chronologically continuous, almost seamless biography, which for long stretches is only interrupted by the insertion of the names of the informants. The whole enterprise is reminiscent of Tatian's Diatessaron, but with the difference that the individual excerpts are more extensive and the source is seldom missing.4
This history of its literary development determines the source criticism of the Cologne Mani Codex. One has to evaluate the separate sources used by the compiler individually.5 In our opinion, you can assume that most of the individual sources were available to the
1 On the small format of the codex see L. Koenen und C. Römer, Der Kölner Mani-Kodex, Abbildungen und diplomatischer Text, Papyrologische Texte und Abhandlungen 35, Bonn 1985, p. VIII fn. 3; L. Koenen, in Das römisch-byzantinische Ägypten, Akten des intern. Symposions, 26-30 September 1978 in Trier, Aegyptiaca Treverensia 2 (Mainz 1983), 93 with fnn. 6 and 106 and Appendix to fn. 6. On the character of the translation of the CMC see A. Henrichs, HSCP 83, 1979, 352f .; L. Koenen, loc. cit. 94 und fn. 8; there is also the assumption that the translation was made in Egypt around the middle of the fourth century (vide infra p. 117 fn. 5).
2 "About the birth of his body" would be a slightly more literal translation, but one that would do less justice to the actual content. L. Koenen no longer maintains his interpretation of the title as analogous to Paul's designation of the church as the body of Christ (ZPE 8, 1971, 249f .; ICS 3, 1978, 164f. and fn. 40 with further literature).
3 Two informants appear p. 74:5f. (Abiesus and Innaeus, who is best known as Mani's second successor, see p. 51 fn. 1), p. 126:3-4 (a "teacher" and a bishop, whose names are not extant) and p. 140:8-10 (a "teacher" whose name has been omitted, and a certain Ana, brother of Zacheas; vide infra p. 101 note 2). Occasionally names are omitted and a whole group of informants is used: 26:6 οι διδάσκαλοι λέγουειν.
4 L. Koenen in The Rediscovery of Gnosticism, Proceedings of the International Conference on Gnosticism at Yale, New Haven, Connecticut, March 28-31, 1978, edited by B. Layton, II (Studies in the History of Religions, Suppl. to Numen 41), Leiden 1981, 734f. and fn. 7; for the literary structure of the codex, which will be discussed briefly here and below, see A. Henrichs in the same volume 724-733.
5 Verification through Manichaean parallel tradition is only possible at the beginning of a quote from Mani's Evangelium (66:4-15; see ZPE 5, 1970, 192-202). For reasons to accept the accuracy of Mani's autobiographical narratives see L. Koenen in The Rediscovery (supra fn. 4) 735f. with fn. 10; A. Henrichs, ibid. 726. On Manichaean biographical writing concerning Mani in Iranian texts see W. Sundermann, Acta Orient. 36, 1974, 125-149.

Codex Manichaicus Coloniensis
compiler in written form.1 This opinion derives from the compiler's working method, which becomes clear from occasional inconsistencies that disrupt the course of the narrative, especially in the links between the excerpts. 2 However, the rarity of such oversights shows that the compiler [p. XVII] smoothed out the transitions. It can also be observed that the biographical narrative is occasionally interrupted by Baraies' theological discussions, which fit into the biographical context only with difficulty, if at all.3 This results, as we readily admit, in an interesting contrast that is a credit to the compiler.
The informants, for their part, report what they, as Mani's disciples, have heard from him. 4 Although Mani spoke and wrote about his life, he is not the author of an autobiography.5 However, he learned from the experiences of Christianity and encouraged his disciples to record his words. In this respect, the problems are different than those with the sayings of Jesus.
Since Mani spoke to his initially very small group of followers and these later became the informants, it is only to be expected that some of Mani's speeches were reported by several [p. XVIII] informants. This explains why excerpts in autobiographical form are occasionally traced back to two sources (cf. fn. 3). In other cases, where Mani had spoken about the epiphanies of the Syzygos, which always follow the same pattern and revolve around the same questions, it must always be expected that the reports of different sources relate the same event.6
The part of Mani's biography that has been preserved for us deals with the period of
1 For the possibility that the compiler also drew on oral tradition, see p.17 note 2.
2 See p. 67 fn. 2 (continued on p. 69), p. 101 (continued on p. 103) fn. 3 and perhaps also on p. 109 fn. 1. In the excerpt from Zacheas (?), which is briefly discussed in the first mentioned note, the discrepancies accumulate. The water of a shallow spring in which Alchasaios wants to wash himself tells him (96:6-11) that it is identical to the water in the lake (or river) and that he nevertheless comes to it to sin against it. In the previous first story about water there is no mention of it being a lake (or a river). The story of baking bread seems to have been greatly abridged (97:11-17). A further indication of the use of written sources could be obtained from the fact that the compiler at one point classifies Innaeus according to his earlier position, not his later position, as head of the Manichaean Church and in doing so probably follows his original. However, there remains an uncertainty in this case in that two different people could be referred to by the name Innaios. See p. 51 fn. 3 continued on p. 53.
3 Pp. 14:(3)-26:(5); 45:1-74:4. At the end of this excerpt from an apologetic homily, the compiler adds a short piece, also from Baraies (possibly from the same homily), which leads back into the biographical context. The excerpt beginning at 140:8 tells the story from the point of view of two later followers. It is also striking that, given the broad presentation of Mani's life with the baptists and his arguments with them, the Indian trip and mission seems to be limited to just 11 pages (pp. 144-154). For this trip, the sources were more sparse, for understandable reasons.
4 The long apologetic homily of Baraies is an exception. However, other excerpts attributed to this source show that he was also a disciple. A different exception can be found at pp. 100:3ff .: one (or two) baptists report how a group of baptists experienced Mani (see p. 101, cont. 103, note 3).
5 According to Baraies, Mani himself, rather than initially his disciples, wrote about his mission (46:1ff .; cf. 63:14-16). Mani will have touched on the topic repeatedly, e.g. in the letter to Edessa quoted in the CMC (64:8- 65:22). Furthermore, the compiler of the CMC also seems to imply written statements by Mani about his life: 46:8-10 πάλιν δέ καί περί της γέν | [νη]c του σώματος αυτοϋ | [έγρα]ψ[εν. The expression anticipates the title of the codex and therefore suggests that the codex contains the ipsissima verba of the founder of the religion. From the Manichaean point of view, the names of the sources guarantee that Mani's words have been transmitted correctly. The quote from Baraies was sufficient to dispel any doubts that might arise.
6 It is at least partly attributable to this fact that it is never clear in the codex whether a distinction is made between the so-called first and second revelations and how the various revelations related to one another.
viii

---

Cologne Mani-Codex
Mani's life from his entry into an Alchasaitic baptist community1 after his third birthday (219/220), his life in the baptist community, finally his departure from this community and his first mission trips up to his audience with King Shapur in the spring of 242 AD.2 His travels first took him to Ctesiphon, then up the Tigris and following the Little or Great Zab in a northeasterly direction to Ganzak in the province of Azerbaijan, from there probably to Armenia (see p. 89 fn. 4) and finally back to southern Mesopotamia. From Pharat he finally embarked for India, probably [p. XIX] because King Ardashir no longer tolerated Mani's religion. In any case, after Ardashir's death, Mani immediately returned to Persia and sought out the new king Shapur in his residence. The rest of the biography has not survived.3 However, it had a continuation: a Coptic church history, now lost, was constructed in exactly the same way, as a compilation of early Manichaean informants, whose names were assiduously recorded as in our codex.4
The codex with its many stories reads like a pious work of entertainment. It tells a story of salvation.5 The conflicts between Mani and the baptists come to life; and the apostle's loneliness and his nuanced relationship with his father appear more complex than one would expect in this type of literature.6 Scholarly interpretation should not lose sight of the popular character of this book. Furthermore, the modern reader will appreciate that Mani, unlike the baptists but in agreement with Gnostics and other heretical Christians, makes a special effort to convert a woman (116:14-120:16). Women could become elect, like men. The codex has further significance for the historian of religion, to whom it shows that Mani grew up in a Judaeo-Christian baptist sect and that his religion, despite all the Iranian details, especially in
1 It is controversial whether and to what extent the baptists among whom Mani grew up can be described as Alchasaitic; see especially the objections raised by G.P. Luttikhuizen (The Revelation of Elchasai, Texte und Studien zum antiken Judentum, Tübingen 8, Tübingen 1985, e.g. p.221). It is certain that in our codex Alchasaios is viewed by Mani as Archegos of the baptists, i.e. their leader or true founder (94:9f.), and that the name of Alchasaios also appeared in a Parthian fragment, i.e. in a tradition independent of the codex (W. Sundermann, Mitteliranische manichäische Texte kirchengeschichtlichen Inhalts, Berliner Turfantexte XI, Berlin 1981, 2.1 line 26, p.19). There can be no argument here; one of these (i.e. counter- arguments) is currently being prepared by R. Merkelbach. At the least it can be assumed that the early Manichaeans passed on Mani's own statements about his youth in a baptist sect that believed itself to be in the Alchasaitic tradition. Our view of the essentially Alchasaitic character of this group of baptists is shared, for example, by K. Rudolph, Antike Baptisten, Sb. Leipzig, phil .- hist. Kl. 121.4, Berlin 1981, 13-17 § 2.5 und L. Cirillo, Elchsai e gli Elchasaiti, Cosenza 1984; see also his contribution in Codex Manichaicus Coloniensis, Atti del simposio Intern. (Rende-Amantea, 3-7 settembre 1984), ed. L. Cirillo and A. Roselli (Cosenza 1986), 97-139.
2 It is possible that the writing was organized into books (see p. 107 note 1).
3 For these journeys, see the introduction to the fourth part of the first edition (ZPE 48, 1982, pp. 1-5).
4 Codex III in C. Schmidt und H. Polotsky, Ein Mani-Fund in Ägypten, SPAW 1933, 27-30; only 8 leaves have survived. Cf. Koenen, ICS 3, 1978, 164f. (In note 37, Koenen wished to find traces of the same citation style in Iranian texts; but the grounds for this were removed after W. Sundermann changed his interpretation of M 4575; Mitteliranische man. Texte kirchengesch. Inhalts [see fn. 12] p. 56 fn. 2 on 4a.1 line 654).
5 A. Henrichs has very well expounded the narrative as a salvation story in his contribution to Codex Manichaicus Coloniensis, Atti (see note 12).
6 E.g. 111:9-116:12; 118:8-120:8. The father is very hesitant to allow his son to convert him. The tension that exists in the relationship between father-son and redeemed-redeemer appears surprisingly lifelike and credible. This is partly the achievement of Mani and the informants, but partly of the compiler who arranged the stories in their current order.
ix

---

Codex Manichaicus Coloniensis
the cosmogony, is primarily an offshoot of the Christianized [p. XX] Jewish tradition.1 The apologist Baraies sees Mani in the light of Jewish apocalypses (48:16-60:12) and, no less accurately, of Paul (60:13-62:22). This definitely reflects Mani's self-image. The stories, which are so pleasant to read and exude a disarming naivety on the surface, are nonetheless theologically significant. They inform us about the life of the elect, and partly also about the life of the catechumen, the lay Manichean who must know how to support the church and its elect. The theological foundations are no less present. We hear about the kingdom of light, the fathers of light and the kingdom of darkness, the dualistic battle between good and evil (e.g. 40:23-41:5), between God and his emanations on the one side and the demons on the other. The cosmogony appears as the foundation of ethics (45:12-23). The two ships of light,2 the separation of the two good and evil natures, (132:13), the three ages (past, present, and future),3 the womb of the pillar through which the souls ascend to the kingdom of light, (34:5- 6) are mentioned almost in passing. The relationship between Nous and body (14:(4)-15:(16); 21:11-16; 115:2-14), and Mani's special position in the work of redemption (e.g. 115:2-15) are discussed. We often hear about Mani's Syzygos, who brought him the revelations and thus reminded him of his heavenly origin and his mission, occasionally also about the Right Hand and its rituals (19: (21)-20:(6)),4 about alms (9:(7)f .; 142:7-8), the resting of the hands (12:(2-6)),5 the forgiveness of sins and redemption (39:3-15) and of gnosis as the separation of the two natures.6 We also find quotations from Mani's letter to Edessa (64:8-65:22) and from [p. XXI] the astonishingly undualistic beginning of Mani's Gospel (66:4-68:5).7 Overall, the Manichaean faith appears in a new, more differentiated and also more humane light.8 2.
A. Henrichs and L. Koenen first pointed out the importance of the new text in a preliminary report published in 1970 (see note 17 above). They then published partial editions in the "Zeitschrift für Papyrologie und Epigraphik";9 this first edition was accompanied by a commentary in three of the four parts. Afterwards, an edition of the entire text in photographs and a diplomatic version were published by L. Koenen and C. Römer.10
The new edition presented here is based on the first edition produced by A. Henrichs

x

---

Cologne Mani-Codex
and L. Koenen; the text is edited by L. Koenen and C. Römer. An English version of this edition, translated by R. Cameron and A. J. Dewey, will soon follow in the Texts and Translations series.1 [p. XXII]
The reader of this new edition will find many deviations in detail from the first edition. Suggestions made by fellow researchers, our own collations of the original and photographs, and renewed consideration of problems relating to the language and the content resulted in more changes than we had expected when we began working on this edition. Even compared to the diplomatic edition (see note 26), new readings emerged. The reader can decide whether we have made any improvements as a result.
The text is presented in long lines, not the short lines of the original; because the short lines would have made it difficult to get an overview of larger pieces of text. On the other hand, the numbering of the short lines in the original had to be retained in order to make the diplomatic and photographic edition usable alongside the new edition. Furthermore, the implied information about the length of the original lines is essential because it determines the length of additions in damaged areas. We hope that the system chosen is self-explanatory as a whole,2 but a few comments are probably in order.
Full lines have an average of 17 letters.3 We used this number as a basis for information about the length of broken line beginnings or line endings. However, where a comparison with adjacent lines makes a letter more or less likely, we have taken this into account. Furthermore, since letters can be written at different distances in the right margin, the reader must expect a certain variation. Where the left edge is missing, the range of variation is small because the left edge of the script forms a vertical margin for all lines on a page.4 On the right-hand edge, however, there could easily have been two letters less or three letters more; however, the number of letters in a full line should not vary more than between 15 and 19 letters. The longer addition is particularly justified if the last letter of the lines is a V; because this can [p. XXIII] always be written as a mark above the previous letter. Much more rarely, kal is written in an abbreviation (K), which takes up little more space than k. However, due to the rarity of this phenomenon, such a spelling can only be expected in exceptional cases.5
The two scribes of the codex structured their text with spaces inside and at the end of lines and with indents at the beginning of sections.6 This results in further irregularities. The first line of a section has an additional letter at the beginning of the line. Spaces inside the line vary between the width of one to five letters. Where such space can be expected in a broken line, the addition may be correspondingly shorter.
The pages contain 23 lines. Pages that are decorated with decorative strips and interlinear spaces at the beginning of a new contributor have only 21 lines. If the headings take up two lines and the decorations occupy more interlinear space, then only 20 lines
1 An English translation of pp. 2-99 has already been published by these two scholars based on the first edition: The Cologne Mani Codex "Concerning the Origin of His Body", Texts and Translations 15, Early Christian Literature Series 3 (edited by B.A. Pearson for the Society of Biblical Literature), Scholars Press 1979.
2 See also the list of editorial brackets and sigla below (p. XI).
3 These and the following figures apply to the two writers of the codex; for the writers see the diplomatic edition, p. XII-XVI.
4 The scribes did not follow a common habit whereby the lines at the bottom of the page always start a little further to the left (so-called Maas's law). However, the verdict is difficult because the pages are more damaged in the lower area and are severely warped as a whole.
5 Cf. p. XVI of the introduction of the diplomatic edition (note 26 above). There are two cases for the end of a line and one case for a correction inside the line. Albeit with great hesitation, we now posit such a spelling inside the line in our addition to 132:14, because this is the only way we have found a satisfactory formulation of the Manichaean formula.
6 We use this word to describe the excerpts as well as smaller sections that give structure to longer excerpts.

Codex Manichaicus Coloniensis
remain on the page (p. 74). But more often the space is saved elsewhere, and the pages in question offer 21 lines (pp. 114, 126, 140). These factors are taken into account in our data on the number of lost lines.
A particular problem with counting the lines arises in three cases in which the page begins with the contributor's name as the heading (pp. 45, 94, and 153). The heading is at the top of the page, where the book title is usually found.1 Since this heading is outside the actual text area, we did not count this heading line. Line 1 begins with the text of the excerpt. In this way we deviate from the practice that we followed in the diplomatic edition on p. 94; there the heading at the top of the page is counted as line 1. This means the line count shifts for the rest of this page. The problem does not affect the other two pages.2 [p. XXIV]
Some other details concern the selection of palaeographic information that we make available to the reader and the structure of the text.
We assume that a reader who wants to engage critically with the text has the diplomatic edition (see note 26) with its photographic plates in front of them, and knows the facts discussed in the introduction there.3 In the edition presented here we therefore omit information that is clearly presented in the diplomatic edition.4 This applies, for example, to the writing of the v at the end of the line with a mark above the line, the abbreviated spelling of kai (K),5 simple corrections by the scribe, punctuation, paragraphs, indents, accents, breathings and other diacritical marks.6 However, extreme consistency in these matters does not seem desirable to us. We have occasionally recorded such information in the apparatus, especially where the text has been partially or even largely destroyed or if, for example, a punctuation or other symbol is decisive in deciding on a particular reading. Of course, information also had to be entered into the apparatus where we changed our reading or interpretation of the traces of the text. We have designated the abbreviations for nomina sacra in the text itself using the usual round brackets (see p. XXXI) because their mere appearance places the text within the Christian tradition.
Information about the rare cases of Iotacist spellings or about other time-saving deviations in the scribe's orthography is not repeated here. They are irrelevant to the text design. The specialist who is interested in the form of writing actually used will in any event profit from using the diplomatic text. For example, only in the diplomatic text will the reader [p. XXV] find that the scribe wrote cixxaveceat in p. 83:9 and ouk' idlove in p. 76:7. Spellings with -xx- occur in the papyri.7 One might even consider tolerating aspirated "Sloc for the text, because such pronunciation is not unknown at the time of the translation of our script. The aspirated spelling of this and similar words is occasionally used not only in the

We have now retained this counting method, although we have ventured a tentative reconstruction of the first lines in the apparatus.
3 We are thinking in particular of the examination of word separation and the accent system in the introduction, pp. XVI-XXIII.

6 We take this opportunity to draw attention to an incomplete statement in the introduction to the diplomatic edition. There it is stated on page XXI that the apostrophe is regularly used after the final consonant of names with apparent non-Greek endings. The exception that confirms the rule is not mentioned: the apostrophe is missing at the end of the line of p. 48:16 (aSau). It is also noteworthy that Evwc (52:9), unlike Evwx' (58: 6 and 9), does not have an apostrophe because the ending doesn't look un-Greek.
7 E. Mayser-H. Schmoll, Grammatik der griechischen Papyri aus der Ptolemaerzeit I 1 (Berlin 1970), 186.
xii

---

Cologne Mani-Codex
papyri, but also, although rarely, in Attic inscriptions of the Hellenistic and later periods.1
In other cases, however, we have taken post-classical development of the language into account in our text format. So we have consistently written avtoû, not avtoû. There is no longer any difference between these two forms, although the full form of the third person reflexive personal pronoun used in the possessive (cavtoo) has not yet completely disappeared.2 Non-reflexive avtoû occurs in the position between the article and the noun, in which the reflexive pronoun or a stressed demonstrative pronoun otherwise appears, e.g. p. 22:5-7 απεστάλην κατά την αυτοϋ γνώμην; p. 54:23 [πολλά ... ]πάρχει ... έν ταΐε γραφαΐς αυτοϋ υποδεικνύοντα περί της αυτού αρπαγής; 57:21-58.1πλεΐα [τα δέ] ... ύπάρ [χει έν ταΐc] avtoû ypapaîc etc. Conversely (and less conspicuously), avtoû can of course also take on reflexive meaning in the position before the article or after the noun associated with an article, e.g. p. 46:8-10 περί της γέν[νη]c του χώματος αυτού [έγρα]ψ[εν].3 The full reflexive form (¿avtoû) sometimes occurs with a reflexive meaning, but not in the corresponding position before the article or after the noun associated with an article, e.g. 48:3-5 kal usyaduval tovc έαυτών (see also 81:14f.) or 81:5 επάν τις καθαρίτη έαυτού την έδωδήν ...
Although we have generally written avtov without a breathing in this situation, exceptions are permitted. Once the scribe himself used the rough breathing to avoid any misunderstanding, as in 72:2-4 αυτός αυτού (αύτου cod.) 4 [τ] ής αποκαλύψεως μάρτυς εγένετο. We did not want to provide less assistance [p. XXVI] than the scribe. In another case, where the pronoun in a literal sense refers to the person addressed, we cannot rely on the authority of the scribe: p. 6:5-6 προς την χρήσιν αυτού "for your use." According to our rule we should have written πρός τήν χρήσιν αυτού. But we wanted to make it clear that, despite its position, autov is the reflexive pronoun, which, as is well known, was already used in classical times for both the first and second person.5 We hope that our inconsistency will facilitate the reader's understanding.6
A detailed examination of the language of the codex is still pending. In the apparatus and in the notes on the translation, linguistic details can only be occasionally referred to if they directly affect the presentation or translation of the text. The editors are aware that their limited, collective reading knowledge of Syriac and Aramaic is completely inadequate for their undertaking. We hope that one day a coherent study of the language and idioms of the codex will be made by someone proficient in Syriac.
Most of the additions are already in the first edition, in the text or in the apparatus. But we have occasionally deviated from it or varied it. As a rule, we have not noted this in detail. The text of the first edition was only included in the apparatus where we were unsure or specifically wanted to point out to the reader the possibility of other reconstructions, occasionally also in places where discussions of individual passages that have since appeared
1 E. Mayser-H. Schmoll, Gramm. (see. fn 40) 175; cf. F.Th. Gignac, A Grammar of the Greek Papyri of the Roman and Byzantine Periods I, Milano 1976, 136 (istoypadoc); W. Crönert, Memoria Graeca Herculanensis (Leipzig 1903), 148 Anm. 2. The phenomenon is rarer in Attic and is hardly associated with general Psi-loss; cf. L. Threatte, The Grammar of Attic Inscriptions I (Berlin-New York 1980) p. 501 und 505f.
2 E.g. 47:6-7 έδειξεν τής έαυτού εκλογήι; see the collection of passages in L. Cirillo, A.C. Mancini, A. Roselli, Codex Manichaeicus Coloniensis, Concordanze (Cosenza 1985).
3 For this phrase, see fnn. 5 and 6.
4 According to what has just been said, the reader will have to take this information from the diplomatic text in the book of photographs (see fn. 3).
5 E. Mayser, Grammatik der griechischen Papyri aus der Ptolemaerzeit I 22 (Berlin und Leipzig 1938, 64); F.Th. Gignac, A Grammar of the Greek Papyri of the Roman and Byzantine Periods II, Milano 1981, 136; W. Cronert, Memoria Graeca Herculanensis (Leipzig 1903), 197 fn. 1. Neither ceavtoû nor other cases of the pronoun appear in the codex, but ¿pavtoû (25:7; also the accusative case).
6 In other places the full first-person reflexive personal pronoun is used, also in possessive usage; so 25:6-7 ¿v φρονήσει της έμαυτού. Cf. the concordance (see fn. 14).
xiii

---

Codex Manichaicus Coloniensis
refer to a now rejected version of the text, and without citing the old reading would have become incomprehensible. Only through this restriction were we able to keep the apparatus within acceptable limits. Anyone who wants to work critically on the text should continue to consult the first edition and its commentary in the future.
There will always be debates about how much to add to incomplete texts. Our critics will find that we have often gone too far, especially in our suggestions in the apparatus, but undoubtedly also in the text. [p. XXVII] On the other hand, such an addition is the shortest way to indicate to the reader what line of thought we were expecting in the lost piece of text. In addition, a proposed addition protects against excessively frivolous suggestions about lost ideas. Anything that cannot be expressed in a linguistically convincing manner in the available space and using the ink traces was probably not there. In this respect, such experimental additions are more than just a vain game. However, they harbour a danger: especially when they are convincing, the reader is tempted to disregard the boundary between tradition and addition. However, we trust that our readers will not misunderstand the meaning of the brackets and the importance of the points with which we indicate the uncertainty of the readings (see the list of symbols p. XXXI).
The same applies to translation. It is intended to show how we understand the text. In order to make the connection of ideas hypothesized by us clear and in order also to offer sufficient help to users who are less familiar with Greek, we have also translated suggestions for additions in the apparatus whenever this seemed to be justifiable. In turn, we trust that the reader will take seriously the square brackets used to mark additions, the many question marks and the occasional translation of alternative textual suggestions in the translation notes. However, we avoided putting square brackets inside a word. The languages are too different for this to make sense; and it is all too easy to give the impression that a supplement is too certain. Conversely, we accept that there are remnants of words in Greek that make our addition more justified than is apparent from the brackets in the translation. Out of similar consideration for non-classical readers, we have also repeated in the translation the information interspersed in the text about the extent of the text that was broken away, as long as the state of preservation made this appear sensible. Occasionally the notes included in the translation also serve to provide factual information necessary for understanding. Within the given framework, we had to be very selective.
The language is often reminiscent of phrases from the New Testament. We have only stated this in a few particularly clear and important places in the text and in the translation in order not to add further burden to the text. Moreover, we would have had equal justification p. XXVIII[ to provide parallels from Manichaean literature. It is sufficient to refer the reader to the commentary sections of the first edition.1
3.
At no time during the preparation of this edition did the two editors divide their responsibilities. In this respect, each of them is responsible for everything. However, C. Römer took it upon herself to collate the original pages in Cologne whenever this became necessary. The German Research Foundation made it possible for her to come to Ann Arbor in the summer of 1986.2 If we had been unable to work together in the same place, it would scarcely have been possible to produce the edition for which we were jointly responsible.
Next we wish to highlight a tool that has provided us with invaluable service in the
1 See also H.D. Betz in 'Paul in the Mani Biography' in Codex Manichaicus Coloniensis, Atti (see fn. 12).
2 The German Research Foundation (Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft) thus continued its support of our research on Mani; see p. XXIV of the introduction to the photographic and diplomatic edition.
xiv

---

Cologne Mani-Codex
work on the edition and which, although it has been partially superseded by the new edition, will still provide the same service to everyone who will work with the CMC: we refer to the concordance, prepared by L. Cirillo, A.C. Mancini and A. Roselli.1
We were supported in our undertaking above all by colleagues and friends. R. Cameron, M. U. Ch. Koenen and L. Pratt read all or part of the corrections. Unfortunately, Albert Henrichs was unable to actively collaborate in this edition due to other commitments. However, he advised us on many details with criticism or approval and also read at least some of the corrections. Furthermore, as explained above, the first edition, which he shared responsibility for, remains our foundation. In this respect, his influence on the current edition and our debt of gratitude to him can hardly be overestimated. Very few scholars have such extensive detailed knowledge and an overview of cultures that are remote to most classical philologists, as well as such a confident feel for language.
4.
We owe our greatest thanks to Reinhold Merkelbach, our teacher and friend. What has just been said about A. Henrich's importance for the current edition applies - Albert will forgive this - a fortiori to R. Merkelbach. Not only did he constantly remind us to keep our promise to make the current edition follow the first edition, but he himself worked tirelessly on the most difficult parts of the text. Some of his suggestions were published in the ZPE2 and some were communicated to us verbally or by letter. Moreover he also worked through a first version of this edition. His criticism and encouragement were equally valuable. In many cases, the text version now proposed is the result of public and private discussions with him.3
We have therefore dedicated this edition to R. Merkelbach on the occasion of his seventieth birthday. However, there was hardly any need for such an external reason. R. Merkelbach is close to both editors; he taught us as students and we have never stopped learning from him. No one has influenced our professional career more than R. Merkelbach. It is significant that one editor is in the prime of her youth, while the [p. XXIX other has begun to count the years remaining in office. It is probably appropriate for the older editor to add that with this gift he wishes to honour his friend rather than his teacher.
February 1988
L. Koenen
C. Römer
Ann Arbor Cologne

More will follow: currently he is not only preparing a response to G.P. Luttikhuizen's book on Alchasaius (see note 12), but also a new edition of the Latin Manichaean Tebesa Codex. The critical apparatus only very imperfectly measures how much our work on Mani is indebted to R. Merkelbach.

Codex Manichaicus Coloniensis
Editorial brackets and sigla
[ ... ] Gap due to external damage to the parchment; the number of dots indicates the number of presumably missing letters; The information is more precise for gaps at the beginning of the line than for gaps at the end of the line because the right margin is uneven. For longer gaps, the dots are arranged in groups of five to allow for a quicker count of the number of letters lost.
Deletion by a scribe; the manner in which the deletion is carried out is not taken into account.
{aß}
Deletion by the editors
<aß> Additions or changes by the editors
(εο) Letters omitted in abbreviations or shortened spellings; In the text, these brackets are only used for nomina sacra; in the apparatus, shortened v and kai without al also appear.
`a' A letter added by a scribe above the line
a A letter, of which the reading seems doubtful to the editors
... Unreadable traces of letters; for the arrangement of the points in groups of five, see above [ ... ].
a, n @ Iota subscript is used where an Iota adscriptum is omitted in the original. No distinction has been made between the hands that wrote the Iota adscriptum. See pp. XIII-XIV of the introduction to the photographic and diplomatic edition.
|
Line changes of the original: the line number of the original is given on every fourth line. If the original ordinal number of the line is not fixed, only the remaining lines are counted and the numbers are placed between round brackets. The following probable equivalents apply to the heavily damaged pages at the end of the codex (see ZPE 48, 1982, 8-9):
pp. 148-151: (1) = 2 153-166: (1) = 3 167-168: (1) = 2
P. 152 which is omitted from this table has no description.
22.1 The numbers in bold indicate the count of the pages recovered.
m1 The first scribe (pp. 1-24; 173-176; 179-182; 184-188)
m2 The second scribe (all other pages)
m3 Collective name for emendators
m3 (corr. prior) the original correction of an emendator
m3 (corr. post) a later correction of the same or another emendator
p(p). Page(s) of the Mani Codex
S(S). Other page references

Cologne Mani-Codex
COLOGNE MANI CODEX (Concerning the Genesis of His Body)
Transs. J.M. and S.N.C. Lieu and M.J. Vermes
(fr. 1) { Fragment of the first extant page of the folio contains seven previous lines among which one could read:} (Perhaps the Syzygos in a vision spoke to Mani): ' ... the fellow-believers ... prophets ... and saviour ... ' {after these, about 39 lines are lost. }
1 { The beginnings of seven lines are preserved, among which this can be read: } ... this same [vision (?) ... ] to me ... it[self(?)] ...
2 { After what remains of one line this follows:} ' ... little by little I (i.e. the Syzygos)] have shown you [what is hidden (?) ... ] from many. You will be able to see that mystery in magnificence and complete clarity.' And then the angel was concealed from me ...
3 { After the end of one line: } ... I was guarded through the might of the angels and of the powers of holiness which were entrusted with my protection. They also brought me up by means of the visions and signs they showed me, which were minor and very brief such as I could bear. For sometimes [he came] like lightning, ...
4 { After very small remnants} ... this I perceived precisely all together. He also gave me a firm grounding in that power which remains steadfast in affliction.
Very many are the visions and very great the marvels which he showed me throughout all that period of my youth. Yet I remained in silence, except ... {only the final letter of the following line is extant. }
5 { From the end of the extant line you can read the following:} ... whilst I with wisdom and cunning wandered in their midst and observed the 'rest' and did no wrong, neither did I cause distress or follow the rule of the baptists or engage with them in dialogue in the way they do.

6 { After seven lines have been completely lost, and the start of another line, one can read: } ... to him: 'Yet you neither take vegetables from the garden nor do you carry wood for his1 use.'
Then that baptist rebuked me saying: 'Get up and come with me to the place where there is wood, take it and bring it.' We went to a palm tree and he climbed it ... {10 lines lost ... } 7 (The same palm-tree spoke to Mani:) ' ... If you spare us the pain, you will not die along with the murderer.' Then that baptist, overwhelmed by fear, leapt out of the tree in confusion and fell at my feet saying: 'I did not know that this secret mystery is in your power. How was the great pain of the palm2 revealed to you?' { ... seven lines lost} ... 8 ... (Mani replied to the baptist)]: 'Why did you take fright and change colour when the palm said this to you? How much more should he, with whom all plants speak, be disturbed?' He was then gripped by wonder and alarmed because of me. He said to me: 'Guard this mystery, tell it to nobody, lest anyone becomes jealous and kills you.' Therefore someone ... {eight lines totally lost } 9 ... One of the leaders of their law spoke to me when he saw that I did not take vegetables from the garden but was requesting them from the leaders as a form of alms. He said to me: 'Why did you not take vegetables from the garden, but ask me for them as a form of alms?' And after that baptist had said to me ...

was greatly disturbed by what he saw and he came and fell before me. When therefore ... someone ... me ...

{After four lines lost: } (Extracts from another witness in which Mani is the narrator:) ... from infancy 11 until my fourth year. Then I entered the religion of the baptists in which I was brought up when I was young, protected through the might of the angels of light and of the greatest powers who had a command from Jesus concerning my protection.1 As they then immediately ... { seven lines lost } ... from the spring of 12 the waters there appeared to me a human form which showed me by his hand the 'rest' so that I might not sin and bring distress on him.
In this way, from the age of four until the time when I reached my physical prime, I was secretly kept safe in the hands of the most holy angels and powers of holiness.
[N.N.] (?) |
{ Six lines completely lost, the first of which contained the name of another witness. The meaning can be identified from the middle of the eighth line:} 13 ... Another time a voice as of the Syzygos addressed me from the air saying: 'Strengthen your power and fortify your mind and accept everything which is to be revealed to you.' Yet again he said the same: 'Strengthen your power and confirm your mind and submit to everything which is about to come to you.' Then I fell ... the voice {after seven lines we read this: } 14 ... 'by the great fathers we have been sent forth.'

Cologne Mani-Codex
his purpose, in the same way the body is nurtured in order to do good.1 ... {after six lost lines this follows: } ... 15 ..... of the place [ ... ] for the 'rest' of the king; and a garment was prepared for him who will wear it. A ship was made ready for a noble helmsman so that he could fish treasures from the sea. The sacred place was founded for the glory of the mind and the most holy shrine for the revelation of his wisdom. But is fulfilled the offspring { seven lines lost } 16 ..... so that by dwelling2 in the body, he might separate those who were enslaved by the rulers, he might redeem and liberate their limbs from subjection to the rebels and from the power of those with authority; and that through it he might manifest the truth of his ownGnosis, and by it throw open the door to those who were imprisoned, and through it offer a good life to them ... {seven lines lost} 17 of all the teachings and all the rules; and so that he might liberate the souls from ignorance by becoming the paraclete and head of the apostolate of that generation.
At the time also when I reached maturity, there suddenly flew down and appeared before me that most beautiful and magnificent mirror image of my face {after seven lines lost among which you might suspect 'and he said again' or similar wording, the theme continues as follows:} 18 ... When I was twenty-four years old, in the year in which Dariadaxar, the king of Persia, conquered the city of Hatra, and in which his son King Sapores assumed the mighty diadem, in the month Pharmouthi, on the eighth day according to the moon,3 the most blessed lord had compassion on me and called me to his grace and sent to me my Syzygos who in great glory ... {8 lines lost} 19 He remembers and informs us of all noble counsels which come from our Father and from the original beneficial good.
1 Adopting the suggestion in the App. Crit.
2 See App. Crit. Translating ίνα χωρίζει | εν οίκήτας
3 I.e. 17/18th April, AD 240.

Cologne Mani-Codex
And he spoke again as follows: 'Even as my Father has been well pleased and has shown me mercy and pity, to redeem me from the error of the sectarians, and showing me compassion through his many revelations he sent to me my Syzygos ... {5 lines lost} ... 20 He (the Syzygos) conveyed to me the noblest hope and redemption for the long-suffering, and the truest counsels and doctrines and the laying on of hands which comes from our Father. Therefore, when he came, he released me and separated me and drew me away from the midst of that sect in which I had been brought up. In this way he called me and chose me and drew me and separated me from their midst. He drew me away to one side ...
{After six totally lost lines one reads: } 21 ... and (showed me) who I am and what my body is, in what way I came and how my coming into this world happened, and who I have become among those who are most distinguished in pre-eminence, and how I was born into this fleshly body, or through what woman I was brought to birth and delivered into this flesh, and by whom I was begotten ... { seven lines lost } ... 22 ... He also showed me how that ... came about, and who is my Father on high or in what way I was separated from him and was sent according to his will, and what command and instruction has been given to me before I clothed myself with this frame, and before I fell into error in this loathsome flesh, and before I put on its intoxication and its habits. He showed me who is my unsleeping Syzygos ... and the ... {after line 18 5 lines lost} ... 23. (And the Syzygos further shows) the secrets and the thoughts, and the pre- eminence of my Father, and concerning myself, who I am, and who is my inseparable Syzygos; moreover, concerning my soul, which is the soul of all worlds, what it is itself and how it came to be. He also revealed to me, in addition,

the measureless heights and unsearchable depths, he showed me everything which ... { After seven lost lines we read this: } ...
24 ... ... ] he who is the most protected: I received him piously, and I obtained him as my own property. I believed him, that he belongs to me and that he is a good and useful counsellor.
I recognised him, and understood that I am he from whom I was separated.
I have borne witness, that I myself am he and am completely the same ... ... { again eight lines lost} ... 25 ... ] to him.
Again he spoke thus: 'With very great skill and understanding I went about in that sect, guarding that hope in my mind while no one perceived who it was that was with me. And I myself revealed nothing about him to anyone during that period which lasted a very long time. Yet I did not ... in the flesh ... in the same way as them ... ' { six lines lost at the end of which there follows } ...
26 I revealed to them none of those things which had happened or were to happen, nor what it was that I knew, or what it was which I had received.
The teachers speak:1
'Therefore when that highly renowned and greatly blessed one revealed to me these great secrets, he began to say to me: "Declare this mystery which I have revealed to you ... to everyone ... and reveal ... " {About 54 lines are missing, eight from page 26 and 46 from pages 27 and 28.}

Cologne Mani-Codex
(Mani is speaking:) 28 .... by means of the scythe 29 which separates the weeds and fruits of the earth, and cuts off the branches of all the rebels, then truth alone is glorified and reigns like the Father1 of the heights. ... { The beginnings of eight lines are preserved; in line 14 is the conjunction "when" in the margin, but the next six lines are totally lost} ... 30 ... to those who are bound in the flesh; but even in this way, little by little I separated myself from the midst of that sect in which I was brought up. I admire beyond measure ... mysteries ... many ... {after the ends of seven lines six lines are totally lost} ... 31 The nations are great in number2, but I am alone. These are rich, but I am poor. How then shall I who am alone against all, be able to reveal this mystery in the midst of the great number which is entangled in error? How will I go to the kings and governors ... good ... race ... to converse ... indeed ... for I am ... and poor and alone ... {after three lost lines this follows:} ... 32 ] very many helpers also associate with him.
As I was considering these things in my mind and examining them, there stood before me immediately my most glorious Syzygos and he said to me: 'Why have you said that this mystery cannot be revealed to the kings by you? So just as I counselled you before as a good adviser, so now I am your adviser in your plan.3 So take courage and prepare to reveal to these ... what has been revealed to you4 ... { two lines lost} ... 33 ..... and for that very purpose you were born.
1 Reading των πατρί του ύψους as per App. Crit.
2 Accepting the conjecture in the App. Crit.
3 32.10-18: 'Why have you said that this ... so now I am advisor in your plan' Translating the reconstructed text in the App. Crit. (n.10) on facing page (viz p. 14).
4 32.19-22 Similarly, translating the text conjecture in n. 12.

Cologne Mani-Codex
people? So that the church will continue to grow, I request from you the whole power of the signs, so that I might perform them with my own hands ... throughout every place, every village and city ... 37 and so that I might bring forgiveness to those who have transgressed; and, moreover, so that no one might surpass me in wisdom, and I might be free of sickness and danger; and that the souls of the victors coming out of the world might be seen by the eyes of all men.1 ... and moreover that when 38 surrounded by oppression or persecution, I might be hidden from the sight of my enemies."
' Then that most glorious one said to me: "Of these gifts which you have asked of me, one of them is given to some of the brothers ... in ... to others ... generation ... were revealed ... to see with the eyes ... the gifts ... 39 ... according to what is fitting for the generation in which you (Mani) were revealed, so that you might bring forgiveness of sins for those sinners who accept repentance from you and rely on holiness, that by setting them free you might bring release from sins and from accusations for your elect, and moreover ... blame ... repentance ... And furthermore ... {among the beginnings of the next seven lines is in line 20 "belief", and after another totally lost line the text continues:} 40 Furthermore, if you ever summon me when you are oppressed, I shall be found standing next to you and shall be your shield bearer in all oppression and danger.
1 There follow the beginnings of 13 lines, four of them written in the margin. Line 11 begins ouoic 8È kaì iva (Similarly in order to ... ). Line 15 begins kaì záliv (and again). In line 17 is ń Sóğa (glory). Line 19 begins kaì iva (in order to) and line 23 begins kaì (and).

Cologne Mani-Codex
"These signs, which you have asked of me, shall be recognised in me so as to be made most manifest to you. For I shall show everything by my hand and be like a mirror to you so that wisdom is perfected in you and you are freed from illness. The signs are given to you through ... { the ends of the next three lines are preserved, the fourth is totally lost} ... 41 the marvels of falsehood, which have been set up to oppose them; for through the signs of truth, those of falsehood have been brought to nothing." After the most glorious one had said these things to me and had strengthened and emboldened me in my apostolate, he disappeared.
'When therefore ... reveal ... when ... similarly { the next three lines are lost} 42 .... and to prune branches from it in order to increase its produce, so that, in this way, starting from a single vine which comes from one superior seed, - if he has plenty of the best earth which is suitable for the vine to grow tall and able to bring forth many shoots - many other vines will grow from this vine ... { there follow the ends of nine lines among which in lines 17-18 appears " ... my father has taken ... " }
{ From line 20 a new section by the same author is indicated. The last line of the page is lost. At the beginning of the next page there is the following:} 43 the height and depth and the 'rest' and the punishment. He revealed to me mysteries hidden from the world which no man could see or hear.
'Therefore, when I had examined everything which I had seen through his ... { the beginnings of 11 lines follow, among which is "having seen"} ...

Cologne Mani-Codex
44 .... which are not given to all, then I immediately separated myself from the ordinances of that religion in which I was brought up, and I became like a stranger and solitary in their midst until the time came when I left that religion.' {the ends of lines 13-19 and decorations follow, which show that the section finishes at line 20}
Baraies the Teacher
45 Therefore know, brethren, and understand everything which has been written here, about the way in which this mission was sent in our generation, just as we were taught by him, and also concerning his body {the first and sometimes the second letters of lines 12-21 are preserved, of which the last is written in the margin; lines 22-23 are totally lost} ... 46 ... this sending1 of the spirit the Paraclete, and after changing sides might say that these men alone have written about the rapture of their teacher in order to boast.
Moreover he wrote also concerning the origin of his body and also ... of that ... {only the last letters of lines 12-20 are preserved; lines 21-22 are totally lost} 47 ... he sins. For let him who is willing hear and realise how each one of the forefathers has made known his own revelation to his own Elect, which he selected and brought together in that generation in which he appeared, and has written about it and passed it on to posterity. One of them declared about his rapture, while those outside were present ... {after the beginnings of lines 19-21 and two lost lines Baraies proceeds to discuss the apostleship of the apostles} 48 ... afterwards to write about
1 The App. Crit. suggests the sense may be "he left writings to his disciples so that no-one would deny ... "

Cologne Mani-Codex
and make known and to praise and extol their own teachers and the truth and hope which was revealed to them. So, therefore, each one of them, according to the course and duration of his mission, spoke about what he had witnessed and has written it as a memorial, including about his rapture.
Thus Adam, first, spoke as clearly as possible in his apocalypse, saying:
'I saw an angel revealed .... 49 in front of your shining face, which I do not know.'
Then he said to him:
'I am Balsamos, the greatest Angel of Light. Receive from me and write these things I reveal to you on the purest papyrus which is not perishable or susceptible to moths.'
(And he spoke) also of many other things which he revealed to him in the vision. For the glory round him was very great. And he also saw the angels and the commanders-in-chief and the greatest powers {only the beginnings of lines 19- 22 survive; line 23 is totally lost} 50 ... ] and Adam became higher than all the powers and angels of creation. And many other things, like these, are in his writings.
Similarly Sethel,1 his son, has also written in his apocalypse, saying,
'I opened my eyes and I saw before my face an angel whose brightness I could not describe for he was nothing other than lightning ...
1 On the Apocalypse of Seth or Sethel see Reeves (1996) 111-40 and on Seth in Gnostic and Manichaean traditions see Klijn (1977) 81-117.
25

Cologne Mani-Codex
51 When I heard these things, my heart rejoiced and my mind was altered and I became like one of the very great angels. That angel put his hand on my right hand and drew me from the world from which I came, and took me to another very large place. From behind me I heard a huge noise from those angels I had left behind in that world in which they belonged .... And I saw ... men ... ' { the beginnings of lines 21-22 follow }
Many similar things 52 were said in his writings, and how he was seized by that angel from a world into another world, and he revealed to him the greatest mysteries of majesty.
Again, in the apocalypse of Enos it says as follows:
'In the third year and in the tenth month I went out to wander in the desert land. I considered in my mind the heaven and the earth and all works and affairs, in what manner and by whom and by whose purpose they came to be ... 53 ..... of death. And he seized me very quietly. My heart was heavy and all my limbs trembled. The bones of my back were shaken by the violence and my feet did not stay firm on my ankles. I went away to many plains and saw there very high mountains. And the spirit seized and took me to the mountain with quiet strength. There many great sights were revealed to me.'
27

Cologne Mani-Codex
Again he said:
'the angel ... and he led me to the 54 northern region, and I saw there immensely great mountains and angels and many places. He spoke to me and said, "He who is all-powerful in supremacy sent me to you, to reveal to you the secrets you have thought about, since you have been chosen for the truth. Write all these secrets on bronze tablets and hide them in the ground of the desert. Everything you write, write very clearly. For this, my revelation which never perishes, is ready to be revealed to all the brothers.""'
Many other 55 similar things are found in his writings which inform about his rapture and revelation. For everything he heard and saw he wrote down and passed on to all later generations of the spirit of truth.
Similarly, Sem also spoke in this way in his apocalypse:
'I was thinking about in what manner all works came to be. And while I was considering, suddenly, the Living Spirit seized me and took me with great force and set me on the peak of a very high mountain and spoke to me saying ... 56 "give glory to the greatest king of honour."'
And again he said:
'The doors were opened silently and clouds were divided by the wind. I saw a glorious throne coming down from the topmost height and a very great angel standing on it. The visual appearance of his face was very beautiful and youthful, more than the shining brightness of the sun, and even than
29

Cologne Mani-Codex
lightning. Like the light of the sun ... 57 of many colours ... a crown woven from spring flowers. And then my facial expression altered so that I fell to the ground; the bones of my back were shaken violently and my feet did not stay firm on my ankles. A voice inclined towards me, and calling from the throne and coming to me it took my right hand and made me stand. It puffed a breath of life into my face and caused the increase of my power and glory.'
Very many other things similar to these exist 58 in his writings, and also the things the angels revealed to him with the instruction to write them for a memorial.
Again Enoch also spoke in this way in his apocalypse:
'I am Enoch the righteous. My grief is great and tears are streaming from my eyes because I have heard the slander which comes forth from the mouth of the godless.'
And he said,
'While tears were in my eyes and entreaty in my mouth, I saw standing before me seven angels who came down from heaven. I saw 59 them and was shaken with fear so that my knees knocked against each other.'
And again he said this:
'One of the angels, Michael by name, said to me: "I have been sent to you for this reason, that we may make known to you all works and reveal to you the land of the pious, and I may show you the land of the impious and what the place of punishment of sinners is like."
He also says,
'They put me on a chariot of wind and brought me to the limits of the heavens. And we traversed the worlds, both the world of death and the world 60 of darkness and the world of fire. And after this they led me
31

Cologne Mani-Codex
into the richest world which was most glorious in light and was more beautiful than the stars I had seen.'
He saw everything and questioned the angels, and whatever they said to him he inscribed in his writings.
In the same way also the Apostle Paul; we know that he was seized as far as the third heaven1 ; as he says in the letter to the Galatians2:
'Paul, an Apostle not of men or through a man but through Jesus Christ and God the Father who raised him from the dead.'
And in the 61 second letter to the Corinthians3 he says:
'I shall come back to visions and revelations of the Lord. I know a man in Christ - whether in the body or out of the body I do not know, God knows - that such a man was seized up into paradise. And he heard secret words which man may not speak of. Concerning such a man I shall boast, but concerning myself I shall not boast.'
Again in the letter to the Galatians4:
'I show you, brethren, that the gospel which I proclaimed to you, I did not receive or learn from man but through a revelation of Jesus Christ.'
As if outside himself 62 he was seized into the third heaven and into paradise, and he saw and heard, and he inscribed this enigmatically about his rapture and mission for fellow initiates of the secrets.
In short all the most blessed apostles, saviours, evangelists and prophets of the truth - each of them observed, as the living hope was revealed to him for proclamation, and they wrote and have handed it down and bequeathed it for the reminding of the future sons of the Holy Spirit who would recognise the perception 63 of his voice.
1 2 Cor. 12:2
2 Gal. 1:1
3 2 Cor. 12:1-5 (but not a precise quotation)
4 Gal 1:11-12 (also not quite the text of the New Testament)

Cologne Mani-Codex
In this way also, it follows that we should write about the apostle most worthy of all praise, through whom and from whom our hope and inheritance of life have come, and tell of him to all later people and members of the faith and the spiritual offspring who are increased through his very bright waters, so that his rapture and revelation may become known to them. For we know, brethren, the exceeding greatness of his wisdom towards us through this coming of the Paraclete of truth. 64 We acknowledge that he did not receive truth from men nor from listening to books, as our father himself says in the writings he sent to Edessa. He says as follows:
'The truth and the secrets of which I speak, as well as the laying on of hands which is mine, I did not receive from men or beings of flesh, nor from familiar writings; but when my most blessed father who called me to his grace and did not wish me and the others in the world to perish, saw and pitied me, he did this to offer well-being to those 65 who were ready to be chosen by him from the religions, then by his grace he took me away from the council of the multitude which did not know the truth. He revealed to me his secrets and those of his undefiled father and of the whole world. He declared to me how they existed before the creation of the world, and how the foundation for all works, good and evil, was laid, and how they manufactured them from the mixture in those times.'
He wrote again and 66 said in the Gospel of his most holy hope:

Cologne Mani-Codex
'I am Mannichaeus, the apostle of Jesus Christ, through the will of God, the Father of Truth, from whom I also came into being. He lives and abides for all eternity. Before everything he is, and he remains after everything. Everything which has happened and will happen, is established through his power. From his person I have my being, and I exist also according to his will. And from him all that is true was revealed to me and from his truth I exist. I have seen the truth of eternity which he revealed. And 67 I declared the truth to my companions; I preached peace to the children of peace; I proclaimed hope to the undying generation; I chose the elect and showed the path leading to the height to those who will climb according to this truth. I proclaimed hope and revealed this revelation and have written this immortal gospel. In it I have put down these pre-eminent secret rites, and I declared great deeds in it, indeed the greatest and holiest of supreme deeds of power. And These things which he revealed I have made known to those who live in accordance with 68 the vision of supreme truth which I have seen, and the most glorious revelation which was revealed to me.'
Yet again he said:
'All the secrets which my father granted me, I have hidden and sheltered from the sects and gentiles and indeed the world, but to you I have revealed them in accordance with the approval of my most blessed father. And if he should wish me to once more, I will again reveal them to you. For the gift which was granted to me by my father is very great and rich. For if the whole world and all 69 men obeyed him, I would be able, with this possession and advantage which my father has given me, to enrich them and render the wisdom sufficient for the whole world.'
37

Cologne Mani-Codex
Again he said:
'When my father showed favour and treated me with pity and solicitude, he sent from there my never-failing Syzygos the complete fruit of immortality, so that he might redeem and ransom me from the error of those of that sect. He came to me and brought to me the noblest hope, the redemption 70 of immortality, true instructions and the laying on of hands from my father. He came and decided and chose me in preference to others and set me aside, drawing me away from the midst of those of that sect in which I was brought up.'
In the books of our father there are very many other extraordinary events similar to these, which make known his revelation and the rapture of his mission. For great is this magnificent coming which comes to us through the Paraclete, the spirit of truth. For what purpose and what reason 71 have we dealt with such things, when we have been convinced once and for all that this mission excels in its revelations? It is because of the reasonings of those who have clothed themselves with unbelief and think nothing of this revelation and vision of our father, that we have repeated from our forefathers their rapture and each one's revelation, so that they may realise that the commission of the earlier apostles was like this. For when each of them was seized, he wrote down and made known everything he saw and heard, 72, and himself became a witness of his own revelation; while his disciples became the seal of his mission.
Baraies the Teacher
As for us, brethren, who as children of our father's spirit have heard and listened to those things, and have rejoiced because of them, let us understand his coming in a spiritual manner: how he was sent by command of his father, and the way in which he was born in bodily form and how there came to 73 him his most holy Syzygos who

Cologne Mani-Codex
separated him from the sect in which his body had been brought up. For in his twenty-fifth year his Syzygos was magnificently revealed to him. For, while he was still under that doctrine of the baptists, he was like a lamb dwelling in an alien flock, or similar to a bird living with other birds who do not speak the same language; for throughout all that time he was brought up always in their midst with wisdom and skill without any of them knowing who he was, 74 or what he had received and what had been revealed to him. Rather, they treated him with the respect due to him as a person.
Abiesus the Teacher and Innaios the brother of Zabed
The lord said: 'When I dwelt in their midst, one day Sitaios, the elder of their council, the son of Gara, seized me by the hand because he loved me greatly and considered me as a beloved son. So he seized me by the hand while no one else was with us, and after a journey he dug up and showed 75 me great treasures which he had secretly stored away. He said to me: "These treasures are mine and I have control over them. From now on they will be yours; because I love no one else like you, to whom I shall give these treasures." When he had uttered these words to me I said in my mind: "My most blessed father has first claim on me, and has given me an immortal and unfading treasure. Whoever inherits this will earn from it immortal life." So I said to Sitaios the elder: "Our forefathers 76 who possessed these earthly treasures before us, and who inherited them, where are they? For behold, they died and perished and did not keep them as their own, nor yet did they take them with them."
'I said to him: "What use are these treasures to me since they introduce errors and faults to everyone who possesses them? For the treasure of God is very great and very valuable and provides life for everyone who inherits it." When Sitaios saw

that my mind was not persuaded to accept the treasures 77 he had shown me, he was utterly amazed at me.'
Timotheos
'Then after a short time I decided to tell Sita and those of his council some of the things my most blessed father had revealed to me and to make known the way of holiness. While I was considering this I had a vision of the whole world which had become like a sea full of very black waters. I saw thousands and tens of thousands sinking in it and going under, 78 surfacing and drifting round the four zones of the sea. I saw in the middle of it a foundation laid which was very high, and on it alone a light was rising and a path was levelled out on it and I myself was walking on this. I turned round and saw Sita holding on to a man who was held by someone else. Sita was plunging into the midst of the sea and darkness, having fallen and gone underwater. I saw only a little of his hair, so I was deeply grieved over Sita; but that man 79 who had thrown him in said to me: "Why are you grieving over Sita? For he is not of your elect nor will he follow your path." Having seen this I did not reveal anything to him. But later when I was sharing the word of truth I saw him contradicting my word.'
Baraies the Teacher
My lord said: 'I had sufficient debate with each one of them in that rule, arising and questioning them about the way of God and about the commands of the saviour, and about 80 ritual ablution and about the vegetables they wash ritually and about their every law and ordinance by which they live.
'When I declared invalid and denounced their teaching and mysteries, showing them how they had not received the things they followed from the commands of the saviour, some of them were amazed at me; but others were angry and in their wrath said: "Does he intend to go over to the Greeks?" Cologne Mani-Codex
I said to them kindly: "There is no value in this ritual washing with which 81 you cleanse your food. For this body is defiled and was formed from a mould of impurity. Consider how, when someone purifies his food and then partakes of it after it has been ritually washed, it is obvious to us that from it come blood, bile, burps and shameful excrement and the foulness of the body. But if someone restrains his mouth for a few days from this food, immediately it is clear that all these shameful and loathsome discharges reduce and are eliminated in the body; but if he then partakes again of food, in the 82 same way they abound in the body again, thus showing that it is from the food itself that they overflow. If someone should partake of food that has been ritually washed and purified, and then partake of that which has not been washed, it is plain that the beauty and power of the body are shown to be the same in both cases. Similarly, the foulness and dregs from both are seen not to differ at all from each other. Therefore, it is not clear that the food which has been ritually washed, when someone has cast it out and discharged it, is any different from the other which is not washed.
"83 And the way you ritually wash yourselves in water every day also has no value. For, if you have once been cleansed and purified, why do you ritually wash yourselves again every day? From this it is evident that you become disgusting every day, and because of the foulness you ritually wash yourselves before you can be purified. Thus, it is very clearly evident that all defilement comes from the body. Behold, you yourselves are clothed in it.
"Therefore, consider yourselves and see what your purity is, for it is impossible 84 to purify your bodies completely; for every day the body is moved and calmed on account of the excretions of the sediment which come from it. Therefore, this thing takes place without the command of the Saviour. However, the purity which is spoken of is the purity through knowledge; it is the separation of light from darkness, of death from life, and of living waters from turbid ones;
45

Cologne Mani-Codex
so you may know that each is different from the others, and you will keep the commands of the Saviour so that he may redeem your soul from destruction and from 85 perdition. This is in truth the most righteous purity which you have been exhorted to practise. You have turned away from it and have separated from it, and have adhered to the purification of the body which is utterly defiled and fashioned from impurity; through which the body coagulated and came to take its shape."
'When I had said these things to them and annulled and denounced what they strove after, some of them were amazed at me and praised me and treated me as leader and teacher. Yet there was much whispering among that sect 86 on my account. Some of them treated me as a prophet and teacher; some of them said: "The living word is sung through him. Let us make him teacher of our faith." Others said: "Has a voice spoken to him in secret and is he saying what it revealed to him?" Still others suggested: "Has something appeared to him in a dream and is he saying what he saw?" Others asked: "Is this the one about whom our teachers prophesied when they said, 'A young man will rise up from our midst and will come forward as a new teacher 87 to call into question our whole doctrine, just as our forefathers have spoken of the 'repose of the garment'?" However, others said: "What he is saying is surely false, does he want to lead astray our people and split the doctrine?" Others of them were filled with envy and wrath and some of them voted for death. Others said: "He is the enemy of our sect". Some said: "Does he intend to go to the Gentiles and eat Greek bread?
47

Cologne Mani-Codex
For we have heard him saying1: 'It is necessary 88 to partake of Greek bread.' Similarly, he says that it is acceptable to partake of wine and wheat and vegetables and fruit, which our forefathers and teachers strictly forbade us to eat. Likewise also he declares the ritual washing which we practise invalid and does not ritually wash himself as we do or cleanse his food like us."
'Then indeed Sita2 and his companions saw that I would not come over to their persuasion, but that I was little by little invalidating and abolishing their rule and their exclusion of food, and that 89 I was not practising ritual washing in a similar fashion to them. When they saw that I opposed them in all these matters, Sita and the majority of his fellow elders held a synod concerning me. They also summoned the steward Patticius and said to him: "Your son has turned aside from our rule and wishes to go into the world. Those things which we prohibit and do not eat, wheaten bread and fruit and vegetables, he does not adhere to and says that it is right to handle them. He transforms our practice of ritual washing. 90 He wants to eat Greek bread". Then, because he saw their great agitation, Patticius said to them: "Call him yourselves and convince him."
'Then they summoned me to them, and once assembled they said to me: "From your youth you have been with us and have lived correctly by the ordinances and customs of our sect. You have been like a modest bride in our midst. What has happened to you now or what has appeared to you? For you are opposing
1 See App. Crit on 87.21
2 Here and four lines below Greek Citav i.e. Sitan, but previously named Sita.

Cologne Mani-Codex
our sect, and invalidating and abolishing our doctrine. You have taken a 91 different path from ours. We hold your father in very great honour: why then do you now declare invalid the ritual washing of our and our fathers' rule by which we have long lived? You are also invalidating the commands of the Saviour. Indeed you want to eat wheaten bread and vegetables which we do not eat. Why do you rebel in this way and not comply with farming the earth as we do?"
'Then I said to them: "May it never happen that I declare invalid the commands of the Saviour. However, if you are reviling me on account of wheaten 92 bread, because I have said that it is correct to eat of it, the Saviour did this, as it is written: 'When he had said the blessing, he provided for his disciples; he said a blessing over the bread and gave it to them'. Was that bread not made from wheat? It also shows that he sat down with tax collectors and idolators1. Similarly, he was invited to the house of Martha and Mary. When Martha said to him: 'Lord, do you not care about me enough to tell my sister to help me?', the Saviour said to her: 'Mary has chosen the good portion 93 and it will not be taken from her'2.
"Observe, therefore, how even the disciples of the Saviour ate food from women and idolators. They did not separate bread from bread or vegetable from vegetable, neither did they eat by earning their living by labouring and farming the earth, in the way you do today. Similarly, when the Saviour sent his disciples everywhere to preach, they did not take a millstone or oven with them; but they ... took one ... ""'
Za[ ... ]
'If then you are accusing me about ritual washing, I shall again show you from your own rule and from the things which have been revealed to your leaders that it is not right to ritually wash yourself.
1 Cf Matt. 9:10-11 and 11:18-19
2 Cf Luke 10:38-42
