Cologne Mani-Codex
'Alchasaios the founder of your sect proves this: when he was going to bathe himself in water, an image of a man appeared to him from the spring of the waters and said to him, "Is it not enough that your beasts abuse me? Yet, you yourself mistreat my home and commit sacrilege against my waters." At this Alchasaios was amazed, and he said to 95 it: "Fornication and defilement and the impurity of the world are thrown at you and you do not forbid it, but you are distressed by me." It said to him: "Perhaps all these did not know who I am, but why did you, who claim to be a worshipper and righteous, not respect my honour?" Then Alchasaios was persuaded and did not bathe himself in the waters.
'After some time he again wanted to bathe himself in water, and he instructed his disciples to find a place which had not too much water, for him to bathe himself. His disciples found the place 96 for him. When he was about to wash, for the second time there appeared to him the image of a man from that spring, which said to him: "We and those waters in the sea are one. Therefore you have come here also to sin and abuse us". Alchasaios in great alarm and agitation allowed the dirt on his head to become dry and so shook it off.'
Again, Mani relates that Alchasaios had ploughs lying ready and went to them. The earth addressed him saying: 97 'Why are you carrying on your work at my expense?' Then Alchasaios took soil from the earth which had spoken to him, and weeping he kissed it and embraced it and began to say: 'This is the flesh and blood of my lord.'2
Again, Mani said that Alchasaios found his disciples baking loaves. At this the loaf spoke to Alchasaios who then commanded them not to bake again.
Again, Mani points out that Sabbaios the baptist was bringing vegetables to the elder of the city. And one particular vegetable wept and said 98 to him: 'Are you not righteous, are you not pure?
1 In this and the next three paragraphs the Greek does not state that it is Mani speaking.
2 Cf Matt. 26:26-27

Cologne Mani-Codex
Why then are you taking us to fornicators?' Sabbaios was alarmed by what he had heard and took the vegetables back.
Again, Mani tells how a date palm spoke with Aianos the baptist who was from Coche, and instructed him to say to my lord: 'Do not cut me down, because my fruits have been stolen. Leave me alone for this year, and next year I shall give you fruits equal to those which were stolen in all the other years.' 99 It also instructed him to say to that man who stole its fruit: 'Do not come this time to steal my fruit! If you do come, I will throw you from my top and you will die.'
Timotheos
'Then I said to them: "Look at these famous men of your sect, who saw these visions, were moved by them and preached them to the others. In the same way I too am practising everything that I was taught by them."
'100 When I was saying this to them and confounding their words, they immediately all became so angry that one of them stood up and hit me. They held me fast in the middle of them and began beating me. They seized me by the hair as they would an enemy. In bitterness and anger they screeched at me as loud as they could, as if I were a heretic, and because of the envy which had overcome them they wanted to strangle me. However, because Patticius, the overseer of the house, begged them not to commit sacrilege against 101 those who were amongst them, they felt shame and let me go. After this assault had befallen me I withdrew myself to one

Cologne Mani-Codex
side, took up an attitude of prayer, and beseeched and begged our lord to support me.
'When I had finished my prayer and was deeply grieving, my most blessed Syzygos - he is my lord and helper - appeared before me. He said to me: "Do not be sad and do not weep!" I said to him: "How can I not be sad? For the men of this religion, amongst whom I have been brought up since my childhood, 102 have changed and have become my enemies because I have separated myself from their sect. Where shall I go then? All religions and heresies are adversaries of the good. I am a stranger and alone in the world. When in this religion, whose believers have read about purity, mortification of the flesh and observance of "resting of the hands", who in addition all know me by name and therefore know the worth of my body better than other religions - for when my body 103 was brought up, cared for and cherished in this religion, I also had contact with its overseers and elders, in relation to the bringing up of my body. So, if these people have given me no opportunity for them to accept the truth, how then will the world, its princes or its teachings, receive me when it comes to hearing these secrets and to accepting these hard precepts? How shall I stand before the kings, princes and authorities1 104 of this world, and the leaders of religions? For, see, they are very mighty and exercise power with their wealth, their authority and their material means; but I am alone and poor in all these things."
'Then the most splendid one said to me: "You have been sent out not only to this religion, but to every people, every school, every town and every place. For by you will this hope be explained
Reading των βα] [ c[ι]λέων και [μεγιστάνων] και τ [αγμάτων τούτου] του κόσμου see App. Crit.

Cologne Mani-Codex
and proclaimed to all zones and regions of the world. In very great numbers will men accept 105 your word. So, set out and go abroad; for I will be with you as helper and protector in every place, where you proclaim everything I have revealed to you. So do not worry and do not be sad."
'Very many were the words which he spoke to me, encouraging me and giving me greater confidence through his hope. I fell down before him and my mind rejoiced at the remarkable sight of that most splendid and sublime one, my most blessed Syzygos. I said to him: " ... what ... 106 For behold, Patticius is an old man; he was shaken, when he just saw one single battle coming my way." Then he said to me: "Set out and go abroad; for, see, two men from that rule will come to you and be your companions. Equally, Patticius will also be the first of your elect and will follow you."
'At that time two young men from the Baptists joined me, Simeon and Abizachias, who had been my neighbours. They came to me, to accompany me everywhere, and they were with me as helpers, wherever we went.'
107 'Thus, according to the will of our lord, I went forward from that sect; in order to sow his most beautiful seed1, to light his brightest candles, to redeem living souls from subjection to rebels2, to walk in the world after the image of our Lord Jesus, to throw on to the earth a sword3, division4 and the blade of the spirit5, to scatter the bread6 on my people and to overcome the immeasurable shame which is in the world and ... 108 I went abroad and into foreign lands, like a sheep in sight of the wolves7, so that through me believers could be separated from unbelievers and be
1 Cf Mt. 13:37
2 Cf Gen. 2:7; 1 Cor. 15:45
3 Cf Mt. 10:34
4 Cf Luke 12:51
5 Cf Eph. 6:17
6 Cf Ex. 16:4
7 Cf Mt 10:16; Luke 10:3
59

Cologne Mani-Codex
chosen, the good seed from amongst the weeds, the children of the kingdom from the children of the enemy' and the descendants of the heights from the offspring of the depths; thus through me he might separate his own from strangers.
At that time therefore I was sent out according to the good will of my most blessed Father to travel about in the world so that creation might be sanctified by me, and so that through me 109 he might make plain the truth of his own knowledge among the religions and peoples, and use me to make a stand before the kings of the earth and the princes of the world in order to take his own from all of them.
In this way I had left, and no follower of that religion had noticed where we went. We travelled about until we crossed and came to Ctesiphon. When I could not get on further, as the water was high2, one of the baptists saw that I was stuck there. When I had left Patticius, I had not said anything 110 to him about where I was going, and so Patticius was sad because of me and wept. He left and visited the surrounding synods; and still he did not find me. He wept and mourned, and the baptists stood by him to comfort him. Then that man who had seen me came to them; and when he saw that they were in mourning he said to them: "Why do you mourn?" They answered: "Because of Mani, who went away and we do not know where he has gone. We fear that somehow someone will harm him." 111 However, he said to them: "I saw him on the bridge, when I went over into the towns." When Patticius heard that he was glad and set off to join me in Ctesiphon.
When he arrived there he did not find me. He went away again and made enquiries. Then he came and found me outside the city in a village called Naser in the assembly of the saints. As soon as Patticius saw me he stepped up, kissed and embraced me, and
1 Cf Mt. 13:25ff; Mk 4:15; Luke 8:12
2 This is the river Tigris. The Greek uses Oax[accnc which means "sea". Koenen and Romer p.77.
61

Cologne Mani-Codex
fell on the ground before me. In tears he said to me: " ... 112 ... ] I was sad, because I thought you were dead and I would never see you again. I said: 'Whom shall I call? Who will listen to me? Whom could I see before my eyes? To whom shall I lament my grief or open the secrets of my heart?' For I had hoped to have you as administrator of all matters now in my old age. For whom can I trust more than you? I see, however, that you will not be with me. I have prayed to God that he will not destroy you! ... of you ... 113 sad ... and for love of you the sighing of remembrance will be planted in my heart. For very sighing for you perhaps I will leave the world.""
Then my lord said to him: 'Do not weep, do not be sad and do not worry about me! For you yourself will be with me. You will find ample grace through me; what you have achieved up to today has happened according to the will of the Father of Light. [ ... 114 ... , ]to be revealed means nothing; for they do not behave as they read in their scriptures.'
Kustaios, the son of the Treasure of Life
Again Patticius spoke to him with tears in his eyes: 'My son, why do you say these things? Just now with your words you have dispelled and cancelled all the doctrine. [ ... ] your friends . . from their midst [ ... ]

as they were in error ... their ... However, the woman was amazed at my wisdom, which not ... to her.
Equally Patticius was surprised; for he had never heard this speech from me. He said in his mind: 'When I heard this teaching, I was moved ... { seven lines only have endings} ... of the humble ... ' 119 They saw an apparition in the shape of a man like an angel of God, so that they were all moved at what they saw. After this that appearance of the man disappeared from before them and like ... I followed. The ... until ...
{After 12 lost lines, some with the first letter and others with none, Patticius speaks} 120 ' ... to reveal this to me. According to God's will I recognised who it is, that is with you.' In the same way that woman stood before me, fell down and said: 'I thank God, who has made [ ... ] but you have come here so that I might see the greatness of the power of God.' { lines 17-23 are either completely or mostly lost}
It is to be assumed that one or more quires with twenty-four pages each are missing here. In the ensuing story Mani has left Babylon and Media and resides in Azerbaijan.
[Witness unknown]
(Mani is giving an account) ' ... but ... I did not stay. From the land of the Medes I went to the brothers in Ganzak. There was at that place a tin mine1. When we came into the town of Ganzak, the followers of our brothers were
1 121.9 Romer (1994) 163 conjectured Muutoc (famine) in place of Mioc. But this would require the deletion of KaTT [ITÉ] | pov ('tin') which appears to be the likely reading. The word tpopfc in the next sentence, supporting the notion of a famine, is also conjectural. As the ensuing story relates to the girl's healing rather than a miracle to do with food, the readings positing a famine are uncertain.

Cologne Mani-Codex
When, however, ... one of the ... {six lines lost} (the hairy man begins to speak to Mani) 128 '" ... Teach me the message of knowledge! For that is why you have come up here." I taught him in [a cave ... ]1, so that wisdom dawned in him. I declared to him about the rest, the commandments and worship before the lights of heaven.
'Again I said to him: "How did you get the hair of your body, which makes you different from all people? ... to this {six lines lost} (the hairy man begins to reply to Mani) 129 ... ] my [ ... ] a tree [ ... ] and I took some of the best fruits. Since that time I have had this hair on my body." As I still stood there he was removed from in front of me and went to that place where I had seen the people. In that place he became a herald of hope and showed the people wisdom.
{six lines lost} (Mani tells of the continuation of his journey) ... 130 ... ] 'I went to a place, which was unknown and cut off from those towns. There too was a large population and many towns. Just as I arrived at that place the heavenly light of the day was rising and the king of that land went out hunting. Then my most splendid and quite infallible leader and Syzygos came to my side and said to me: " { five lines lost} 131 ... not far from the king and his princes ... When the king and his princes saw me, they became agitated and astonished ... He beckoned me to come to him2 so that ... to them ... When I had got nearer to them,
1 128,7 'in [a cave ... ]' (εν τωι [σπηλαίων): new reading suggested by Romer (1994) 164.
2 131,7-9 'He beckoned me to come to him' (έ[νευτεν] δέ μοι ώς έκ τ[ ..... .. ] προς αυτούς άφ[ικνοίμη(ν)]): based on one of a series of new readings suggested by Römer (1994) 164.

the king and his princes got down off their chariots. I stood before him and bowed in obeisance! ... {five lines lost } ... 132 to him. Before him I explained wisdom and the commandments and declared everything to him.
'For many days, when I was staying there, he himself with his princes sat before me. I showed them the separation of the two natures and the doctrine of beginning, middle and end.
'{seven lines lost, of which the first two show faint traces} 133 ... He accepted everything that he had heard ... from me. After his splendid work was complete through me, while the king was sitting on the throne and his princes were before him, the most magnificent one suddenly flew down and stepped up to me, and ... to me. The king and his princes ... { seven lines lost} ...
'134 Then the king grew strong in wisdom and he was grounded in knowledge and faith. Similarly also his princes became full of faith. The king accepted joyfully the commandments which I had taught him, and ordered that they should be explained and declared throughout his kingdom ... place ... this and ... after ... { six lines lost and the seventh has very faint traces }

'Then the most splendid one took me to that place where he had parted me from Patticius. When he saw me he was very happy, he kissed me and said to me: "Where did you go? ... leaving me alone?"
'{After line 16, eight lines are lost} (In the presence of Mani it seems Patticius is relating a vision and that he is addressing the Syzygos) 136 ... "For I (Patticius) saw with my own eyes his strong power exceeding all visions that I might usually expect." So ... My lord said to him: "You are one of his own." Then he took him to places very high up and said: "I have done this according to the will of your father; he himself sent me to you".'

he was defeated and brought laughter on himself, so that he was filled with envy and malice. In Pharat there was a man called Oggias, who was famous because of his influence and his power over the men.3 I4 saw how the merchants, who were preparing to travel by ship as far as the Persians and Indians, sealed his wares, and did not put to sea, until he came on board. ... When Oggias ... {the next eight lines are nearly totally lost} (Mani is apparently talking with Oggias) ... "145 ... him ... to you." Then he said to me: "I want to embark on a ship and travel to the Indians so that I receive ... if I should so [succeed ... "]5 I said to him however: "I ... you ... "' {Nine lines are missing of which the first two have faint traces }

2 143, 12-15: 'in contrast to our teachers'. έν διαφ[οράι πρός τούς] | τών ήμετέρων διδασκάλων λόγους is a new reading suggested by Römer (1994) 165. ("In contrast with the words of our teachers") But this would be an unnatural use of mpòc.
3 144,5-6 Römer (1994) 165 suggests a probable alternative may be: 'a man who was [eminent] for his [philanthropy]' (άνθρωπος επί | [δοξος έλπί της αυτοϋ | [ευτυχίαι] ).

5 145.9: 'if [I should] so [succeed ... "]’ (επαν ού[τως προχωρή μοι]): new reading suggested by Romer (1994) 165.
